```text
method.tex:7:\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}.}
method.tex:8:\label{fig:pipeline}
experiments.tex:12:\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}
experiments.tex:13:\label{fig:label-decomposition}
experiments.tex:23:\caption{Per-task death rate differential vs.\ performance gap ($n{=}11$ task types). The near-perfect correlation ($r = 0.97$) is a structural consequence of death dominance (DAF $\to 1 \Rightarrow r \to 1$), not an independent finding. The sole outlier (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, red diamond) has zero deaths; its $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ arises entirely from non-death score differences.}
experiments.tex:24:\label{fig:decomposition}
experiments.tex:34:\caption{Action sequences for 6 representative death episodes. The fatal \texttt{focus on} (arrow) appears in final steps after long \texttt{connect} sequences (``connect loop $\to$ fatal focus'').}
experiments.tex:35:\label{fig:death-timeline}
experiments.tex:71:\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded). No intervention approaches deterministic-level safety.}
experiments.tex:72:\label{tab:agent-improvement}
experiments.tex:85:\caption{Death rate vs.\ trial count for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Reflexion drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus; Retry is nearly flat. Horizontal lines: 72B ($30.6\%$) and deterministic ($16.3\%$) baselines. Error bands: Wilson 95\% CIs.}
experiments.tex:86:\label{fig:dose-response}
experiments.tex:103:\caption{Cross-environment label distributions. ScienceWorld and ALFWorld both favor deterministic via different mechanisms (death avoidance vs.\ information asymmetry); MATH shows no routing signal.}
experiments.tex:104:\label{fig:signal-reversal}
experiments.tex:126:\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes). $\dagger$\,Single seed (42); see Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness.}
experiments.tex:127:\label{tab:baselines}
main.tex:95:\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}
main.tex:96:\label{alg:labeling}
main.tex:155:\caption{Per-task decomposition of the death avoidance effect. D\%: death rate for internal (int) and deterministic (det) strategies. D/I/N: episode labels (det-preferred / internal-preferred / no-preference). Sorted by $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ descending. The \textit{All} row is an episode-weighted aggregate across 245 episodes. $\dagger$\,Only task with zero deaths under both strategies; $n{=}40$ because data was collected in two batches, with the second batch adding 20 episodes to increase statistical power for this zero-death outlier.}
main.tex:156:\label{tab:per-task}
main.tex:197:\caption{Death-rate analysis of all 19 excluded task types. D\%: death rate (score $\leq -100$). $\Delta$DR = D\%\textsubscript{int} $-$ D\%\textsubscript{det}. 18 of 19 excluded tasks (95\%) exhibit positive death asymmetry ($\Delta$DR $\geq +20$\,pp). Exclusion groups: A = development set (excluded to prevent circular evaluation), B = taxonomically redundant with an included family representative, C = agent-floor tasks (backbone achieves near-zero progress regardless of strategy). The mean $\Delta$DR among excluded tasks ($+38.2$\,pp) exceeds that of included tasks ($+28.7$\,pp), confirming that task selection does not bias toward death-prone tasks. Group B--C estimates are based on $n{=}5$ prescreening episodes per task and should be interpreted as directional evidence rather than precise rate estimates.}
main.tex:198:\label{tab:excluded-death}
main.tex:226:\caption{Leave-one-out Pearson $r$ for the death rate differential vs.\ $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ correlation. Full $r = 0.970$; LOO range $[0.963, 0.980]$. No single task type is a leverage point.}
main.tex:227:\label{tab:loo}
main.tex:251:\caption{Cluster-level tests of death-rate asymmetry ($K{=}11$ task clusters, 245 episodes). 10 of 11 clusters show internal DR $>$ deterministic DR; the sole exception (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}) has zero deaths under both strategies. Cohen's $d = 3.02$. The exact permutation $p = 0.002$ ($4/2{,}048$) reflects the near-unanimous directional consistency.}
main.tex:252:\label{tab:wild-cluster}
main.tex:272:\caption{Leave-one-task-out DAF sensitivity (penalty-independent label fraction). Among the 10 death-prone tasks, removing any single task changes DAF by at most 3.0\,pp (range: 75.9\%--78.9\%). Removing \emph{Find Non-Living Thing}, the sole death-free task that contributes all 20 score-comparison episodes, raises DAF to 100\% by construction.}
main.tex:273:\label{tab:loto-daf}
main.tex:308:\caption{Per-task death rates (\%) for Reflexion at $N{=}3$, 4, 5 on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}20$ per task). Aggregate death rates confirm stabilization at $N \geq 3$ (range: $34.0$--$36.5\%$).}
main.tex:309:\label{tab:dose-response-detail}
main.tex:363:\caption{Router training hyperparameters. Both architectures use the same base model with LoRA adaptation. The classification head degenerates across all configurations; the implicit router results are reported as 4-seed mean$\pm$std.}
main.tex:364:\label{tab:hyperparams}
main.tex:418:\caption{Per-seed router performance under cross-split evaluation (8 seeds, generation-based, \texttt{enable\_thinking=False}). Step-level evaluation allows steps from the same episode in both train and validation sets (same-episode leakage). Episode-level evaluation holds out entire episodes; the resulting $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ is below the 50\% chance baseline. A text augmentation ablation (Table~\ref{tab:text-augmentation}) confirms this failure persists under the current data distribution and augmentation regime.}
main.tex:419:\label{tab:seed-robustness}
main.tex:442:\caption{Text augmentation ablation: episode-level balanced accuracy across training set sizes (25\%--800\% of original via paraphrase, $32{\times}$ range). Internal recall $= 0.0$ at all scales (complete majority-class collapse). Det\%: fraction of deterministic-preferred labels in training data. The 50\% scale is a single seed; all others report mean$\pm$std across seeds.}
main.tex:443:\label{tab:text-augmentation}
main.tex:475:\caption{Sensitivity of DAF to death penalty magnitude $P$. DAF$(P) = 1 - \Delta_{\text{nd}} / \Delta_{\text{full}}(P)$, where $\Delta_{\text{full}}(P) \approx \delta \cdot P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}$, $\delta = 0.2653$ is the death-rate differential, and $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (strict neither-died filtering). Recomputed from 245 episode-level data; bootstrap 95\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$). \textsuperscript{*}Default setting.}
main.tex:476:\label{tab:penalty-sensitivity}
main.tex:500:\caption{Pearson $r$ between per-task death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{full}}(P)$ across penalty magnitudes. The correlation is non-significant at low penalties and emerges at $P \geq 50$, which reflects the transition from non-death-dominated to death-dominated score gaps. \textsuperscript{*}Default ScienceWorld setting.}
main.tex:501:\label{tab:penalty-correlation}
main.tex:524:\caption{Cross-environment label distribution. ``Deterministic'' denotes qualitatively different computation modes: ScienceWorld = oracle simulator with ground-truth state; MATH = Python code interpreter; ALFWorld = admissible-action enumeration. $\dagger$\,ALFWorld's deterministic advantage stems from information asymmetry, not death avoidance.}
main.tex:525:\label{tab:cross-env}
main.tex:548:\caption{Per-task-type decomposition for ALFWorld (135 games, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct). D/I/N: deterministic-preferred / internal-preferred / no-preference. No death mechanism exists; $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ is driven entirely by information asymmetry from admissible-action access. Sorted by $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ descending.}
main.tex:549:\label{tab:alfworld-pertask}
main.tex:587:\caption{Per-task Llama-3.1-8B cross-model replication ($N{=}20$ per task). D/I/N: deterministic-preferred / internal-preferred / no-preference. Sorted by $\Delta$ DR descending.}
main.tex:588:\label{tab:cross-model-detail}
main.tex:614:\caption{Cross-model and cross-environment replication summary. \textbf{ScienceWorld}: death dominance replicates across three model families in the 7--9B range and intensifies under $10{\times}$ scaling (two 70B-class models, DAF $92.4$--$101.0\%$); DAF 95\% CIs from non-clustered episode-level bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). \textbf{ALFWorld}: DAF $= 0\%$ (no death mechanism); routing signal arises from information asymmetry. \textbf{MATH}: no routing signal without catastrophic failure. Signal column: diagnosed routing mechanism. DR = death rate. Gemma-2's wider CI reflects one zero-death task type (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}); excluding it yields DAF $= 99.9\%$ $[99.7, 100.0]$.}
main.tex:615:\label{tab:cross-model-env}
main.tex:624:\label{tab:death-taxonomy}
main.tex:635:\caption{Action distribution for the internal (LLM) strategy in death ($n{=}105$) vs.\ non-death ($n{=}140$) episodes. Left: full distribution (both dominated by \texttt{connect}). Right: non-\texttt{connect} actions zoomed. \texttt{focus on} is moderately more frequent in death episodes ($5.4\%$ vs.\ $3.4\%$), while non-death episodes show higher rates of observation and object interaction.}
main.tex:636:\label{fig:death-action-dist}
main.tex:644:\caption{Non-\texttt{connect} action distribution comparison between internal (left) and deterministic (right) strategies. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different action selection within those states.}
main.tex:645:\label{fig:death-comparison}
main.tex:684:\caption{Capacity scaling: step-level and episode-level balanced accuracy (4 seeds, identical configuration across all scales). Step-level accuracy is flat ($56$--$59\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B breaks the random baseline ($59.0\%$, $p < 0.005$).}
main.tex:685:\label{tab:capacity}
main.tex:716:\caption{Death-risk threshold routing analysis on 245 episodes. The optimal threshold routes only \texttt{find-non-living-thing} (the sole zero-death task) to internal, with all other tasks routed to deterministic. Bootstrap 95\% CI for the difference: $[-8.87, +5.92]$.}
main.tex:717:\label{tab:threshold-routing}
main.tex:752:\caption{Retrospective validation of the decision framework. All four conditions are correctly classified (4/4).}
main.tex:753:\label{tab:decision-framework}
main.tex:782:\caption{Extended validation ($n{=}20$) of representative excluded task types. All five tasks confirm death asymmetry; mean $\Delta$DR $= +32.0$\,pp is consistent with the $n{=}5$ prescreening estimate for these same tasks (Table~\ref{tab:excluded-death}).}
main.tex:783:\label{tab:extended-val}
main.tex:921:\caption{Non-death non-tie episode classification. Hallucination-induced: agent's state model is incorrect (e.g., believing \texttt{eat} completes the task). Object selection quality: both strategies succeed but choose different-value objects. Action selection: correct state model but suboptimal action sequences.}
main.tex:922:\label{tab:non-death-taxonomy}

The correlation is non-significant at low penalties and emerges at $P \\geq 50$, which reflects the transition from non-death-dominated to death-dominated score gaps. \\textsuperscript{*}Default ScienceWorld setting.}\nmain.tex:501:\\label{tab:penalty-correlation}\nmain.tex:524:\\caption{Cross-environment label distribution. ``Deterministic'' denotes qualitatively different computation modes: ScienceWorld = oracle simulator with ground-truth state; MATH = Python code interpreter; ALFWorld = admissible-action enumeration. $\\dagger$\\,ALFWorld's deterministic advantage stems from information asymmetry, not death avoidance.}\nmain.tex:525:\\label{tab:cross-env}\nmain.tex:548:\\caption{Per-task-type decomposition for ALFWorld (135 games, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct). D/I/N: deterministic-preferred / internal-preferred / no-preference. No death mechanism exists; $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ is driven entirely by information asymmetry from admissible-action access. Sorted by $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ descending.}\nmain.tex:549:\\label{tab:alfworld-pertask}\nmain.tex:587:\\caption{Per-task Llama-3.1-8B cross-model replication ($N{=}20$ per task). D/I/N: deterministic-preferred / internal-preferred / no-preference. Sorted by $\\Delta$ DR descending.}\nmain.tex:588:\\label{tab:cross-model-detail}\nmain.tex:614:\\caption{Cross-model and cross-environment replication summary. \\textbf{ScienceWorld}: death dominance replicates across three model families in the 7--9B range and intensifies under $10{\\times}$ scaling (two 70B-class models, DAF $92.4$--$101.0\\%$); DAF 95\\% CIs from non-clustered episode-level bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). \\textbf{ALFWorld}: DAF $= 0\\%$ (no death mechanism); routing signal arises from information asymmetry. \\textbf{MATH}: no routing signal without catastrophic failure. Signal column: diagnosed routing mechanism. DR = death rate. Gemma-2's wider CI reflects one zero-death task type (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}); excluding it yields DAF $= 99.9\\%$ $[99.7, 100.0]$.}\nmain.tex:615:\\label{tab:cross-model-env}\nmain.tex:624:\\label{tab:death-taxonomy}\nmain.tex:635:\\caption{Action distribution for the internal (LLM) strategy in death ($n{=}105$) vs.\\ non-death ($n{=}140$) episodes. Left: full distribution (both dominated by \\texttt{connect}). Right: non-\\texttt{connect} actions zoomed. \\texttt{focus on} is moderately more frequent in death episodes ($5.4\\%$ vs.\\ $3.4\\%$), while non-death episodes show higher rates of observation and object interaction.}\nmain.tex:636:\\label{fig:death-action-dist}\nmain.tex:644:\\caption{Non-\\texttt{connect} action distribution comparison between internal (left) and deterministic (right) strategies. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \\texttt{focus on} rates (${\\sim}5\\%$); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different action selection within those states.}\nmain.tex:645:\\label{fig:death-comparison}\nmain.tex:684:\\caption{Capacity scaling: step-level and episode-level balanced accuracy (4 seeds, identical configuration across all scales). Step-level accuracy is flat ($56$--$59\\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B breaks the random baseline ($59.0\\%$, $p < 0.005$).}\nmain.tex:685:\\label{tab:capacity}\nmain.tex:716:\\caption{Death-risk threshold routing analysis on 245 episodes. The optimal threshold routes only \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} (the sole zero-death task) to internal, with all other tasks routed to deterministic. Bootstrap 95\\% CI for the difference: $[-8.87, +5.92]$.}\nmain.tex:717:\\label{tab:threshold-routing}\nmain.tex:752:\\caption{Retrospective validation of the decision framework. All four conditions are correctly classified (4/4).}\nmain.tex:753:\\label{tab:decision-framework}\nmain.tex:782:\\caption{Extended validation ($n{=}20$) of representative excluded task types. All five tasks confirm death asymmetry; mean $\\Delta$DR $= +32.0$\\,pp is consistent with the $n{=}5$ prescreening estimate for these same tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:excluded-death}).}\nmain.tex:783:\\label{tab:extended-val}\nmain.tex:921:\\caption{Non-death non-tie episode classification. Hallucination-induced: agent's state model is incorrect (e.g., believing \\texttt{eat} completes the task). Object selection quality: both strategies succeed but choose different-value objects. Action selection: correct state model but suboptimal action sequences.}\nmain.tex:922:\\label{tab:non-death-taxonomy}",

      "content": "    \\text{SCI} = \\frac{\\sum_{i=1}^K \\sum_{j=1}^K |c_i - c_j|}{2K \\sum_{k=1}^K c_k}\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\section{Labeling Quality Audit}\n\\label{app:labeling}\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$",
      "numLines": 30,
      "startLine": 88,
      "totalLines": 938

1	\section{Method}

3

5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}

8	\label{fig:pipeline}
9	\end{figure}

11	Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\% confidence intervals for all metrics.
12	
13	\subsection{Problem Setup}
14	\label{sec:problem-setup}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01U66vtAf3ZewEF1UpCpk62K`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	In ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.
7	
8	We propose a \emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Always-deterministic matches 96.3\% of oracle decisions, capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points (\S\ref{sec:baselines}); removing death episodes reveals that oracle access provides almost no non-death advantage ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 9.6\% of total gap; \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}). Two independent 70B-class models yield DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\%$ (Llama-70B exceeds 100\%: death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap), and Reflexion ($N{=}3$) similarly drives DAF above 100\% (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
9	
10	This addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\% tie rate; \S\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\S\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \citep{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.
11	
12	Cross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
13	An exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
14	
15	Our contributions: (1)~an \textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\%$), four interventions, $10{\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:cross-model}).

      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nIn ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \\citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \\citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \\citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \\citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Always-deterministic matches 96.3\\% of oracle decisions, capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}); removing death episodes reveals that oracle access provides almost no non-death advantage ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 9.6\\% of total gap; \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}). Two independent 70B-class models yield DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\\%$ (Llama-70B exceeds 100\\%: death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap), and Reflexion ($N{=}3$) similarly drives DAF above 100\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\n\nThis addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\\% tie rate; \\S\\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\citep{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.\n\nCross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\\%$), four interventions, $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n\n",
      "numLines": 18,

    }

1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}
7	\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}.}
8	\label{fig:pipeline}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\% confidence intervals for all metrics.
12	
13	\subsection{Problem Setup}
14	\label{sec:problem-setup}
15	
16	Consider an interactive environment with observation space $\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\pi: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$: \textbf{internal} (${\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \textbf{deterministic} (${\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \S\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \emph{routing policy} $\rho: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\rho$.
17	
18	\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}
19	\label{sec:collection}
20

Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \\S\\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. For context: DAF $= 50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$, $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$, and $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (full curve in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). We treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic and DAF as a supplementary, environment-specific measure. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}\nDAF (90.4\\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \\gg$ typical non-death $\\Delta{\\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction, invariant to penalty magnitude, serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n",

Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nIn ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \\citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \\citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \\citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \\citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Always-deterministic matches 96.3\\% of oracle decisions, capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}); removing death episodes reveals that oracle access provides almost no non-death advantage ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 9.6\\% of total gap; \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}). Two independent 70B-class models yield DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\\%$ (Llama-70B exceeds 100\\%: death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap), and Reflexion ($N{=}3$) similarly drives DAF above 100\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\n\nThis addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\\% tie rate; \\S\\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\citep{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.\n\nCross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\\%$), four interventions, $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n\n",

93	
94	\begin{algorithm}[t]
95	\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}
96	\label{alg:labeling}
97	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
98	\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$
99	\ENSURE Label $\ell \in \{{\tt det\text{-}pref}, {\tt int\text{-}pref}, {\tt no\text{-}pref}\}$
100	\FOR{each $m \in \mathcal{M}$}
101	    \STATE Run episode $\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$
102	    \STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$
103	\ENDFOR
104	\IF{$d_{\tt int} = 1$ \AND $d_{\tt det} = 0$}
105	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt det\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{death asymmetry}
106	\ELSIF{$d_{\tt int} = 0$ \AND $d_{\tt det} = 1$}
107	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt int\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{death asymmetry}
108	\ELSIF{$d_{\tt int} = d_{\tt det} = 1$}
109	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt no\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{both dead}
110	\ELSIF{$s_{\tt det} > s_{\tt int}$}
111	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt det\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{score comparison}
112	\ELSIF{$s_{\tt int} > s_{\tt det}$}
113	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt int\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{score comparison}
114	\ELSE
115	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt no\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{tied alive}
116	\ENDIF
117	\RETURN $\ell$
118	\end{algorithmic}
119	\smallskip
120	\noindent\textit{Assumption:} The death-first priority (lines 5--10) is valid when $P$ exceeds the maximum partial score, guaranteeing that a dead episode never outscores a surviving one. ScienceWorld satisfies this ($P{=}100$, max partial score ${=}100$).\\
121	\noindent\textit{Note:} When both strategies die (line 9), we assign \texttt{no\_preference}; in ScienceWorld, death yields a fixed $-100$ regardless of timing, so both-dead episodes contribute no routing signal.
122	\end{algorithm}
```

25	    \bigl(s^{\tt int}, s^{\tt det}, d^{\tt int}, d^{\tt det}\bigr) \triangleq \\
26	    \bigl(s(\tau_{\tt int}),\, s(\tau_{\tt det}),\, d(\tau_{\tt int}),\, d(\tau_{\tt det})\bigr)
27	\end{multline}
28	
29	We use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \S\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\ref{sec:cross-env}.
30	
31	\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}
32	\label{sec:labeling}
33	
34	We define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:
35	\begin{multline}
36	    L\!\bigl(\tau_{\tt int}^{(t)}, \tau_{\tt det}^{(t)}\bigr) \;\rightarrow \\
37	    \ell \in \{{\tt det\text{-}pref},\; {\tt int\text{-}pref},\; {\tt no\text{-}pref}\}
38	\end{multline}
39	
40	The labeling function is \emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \texttt{no\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.
41	
42	\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}
43	\label{sec:daf}
44

Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.",
    "newString": "The labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textit{Assumption:} The death-first priority (lines 5--10) is valid when $P$ exceeds the maximum partial score, guaranteeing that a dead episode never outscores a surviving one. ScienceWorld satisfies this ($P{=}100$, max partial score ${=}100$).\\\\\n\\noindent\\textit{Note:} When both strategies die (line 9), we assign \\texttt{no\\_preference}; in ScienceWorld, death yields a fixed $-100$ regardless of timing, so both-dead episodes contribute no routing signal.\n\\end{algorithm}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \\S\\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. For context: DAF $= 50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$, $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$, and $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (full curve in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). We treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic and DAF as a supplementary, environment-specific measure. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}\nDAF (90.4\\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \\gg$ typical non-death $\\Delta{\\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction, invariant to penalty magnitude, serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n",

    "oldString": "\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textit{Assumption:} The death-first priority (lines 5--10) is valid when $P$ exceeds the maximum partial score, guaranteeing that a dead episode never outscores a surviving one. ScienceWorld satisfies this ($P{=}100$, max partial score ${=}100$).\\\\\n\\noindent\\textit{Note:} When both strategies die (line 9), we assign \\texttt{no\\_preference}; in ScienceWorld, death yields a fixed $-100$ regardless of timing, so both-dead episodes contribute no routing signal.\n\\end{algorithm}",

          " \\label{app:labeling}",
          " ",

          "-\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}",
          "-\\label{alg:labeling}",
          "-\\begin{algorithmic}[1]",
          "-\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$",
          "-\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$",
          "-\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}",
          "-    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$",
          "-    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$",
          "-\\ENDFOR",
          "-\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}",

          "-    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}",
          "-\\ELSE",
          "-    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}",

          "-\\noindent\\textit{Assumption:} The death-first priority (lines 5--10) is valid when $P$ exceeds the maximum partial score, guaranteeing that a dead episode never outscores a surviving one. ScienceWorld satisfies this ($P{=}100$, max partial score ${=}100$).\\\\",
          "-\\noindent\\textit{Note:} When both strategies die (line 9), we assign \\texttt{no\\_preference}; in ScienceWorld, death yields a fixed $-100$ regardless of timing, so both-dead episodes contribute no routing signal.",

          " ",
          " Note that episode-level labels are used to evaluate step-level predictions, introducing a granularity mismatch rather than independent validation. A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine computation mode differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference} labels (61.2\\%), 115 (46.9\\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\\%) are both-dead, all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields step-level balanced accuracy of 57.3\\%, within 1$\\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\\pm$ 0.5\\%, step-level Trainer evaluation); score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. (Step-level metrics are used here for the relative comparison; as shown in \\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}, absolute generalization to new episodes is near chance.) The 97.4\\% step-level tie rate (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.",
          " "
        ]
      }

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
  "old_string": "\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textit{Assumption:} The death-first priority (lines 5--10) is valid when $P$ exceeds the maximum partial score, guaranteeing that a dead episode never outscores a surviving one. ScienceWorld satisfies this ($P{=}100$, max partial score ${=}100$).\\\\\n\\noindent\\textit{Note:} When both strategies die (line 9), we assign \\texttt{no\\_preference}; in ScienceWorld, death yields a fixed $-100$ regardless of timing, so both-dead episodes contribute no routing signal.\n\\end{algorithm}",
  "new_string": ""
}

89	\end{equation}
90	
91	\section{Labeling Quality Audit}
92	\label{app:labeling}
93	
94	
95	Note that episode-level labels are used to evaluate step-level predictions, introducing a granularity mismatch rather than independent validation. A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine computation mode differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \texttt{no\_preference} labels (61.2\%), 115 (46.9\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\%) are both-dead, all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields step-level balanced accuracy of 57.3\%, within 1$\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\pm$ 0.5\%, step-level Trainer evaluation); score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. (Step-level metrics are used here for the relative comparison; as shown in \S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}, absolute generalization to new episodes is near chance.) The 97.4\% step-level tie rate (\S\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.
96	
97	\section{Per-Task Decomposition}
98	\label{app:per-task}

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "3be4b042-a863-449d-9834-d39e84b012c0",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "404cbe1b-bec9-439a-b3f8-8d96e1919b94",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "af745212-b94f-4753-8b0c-83ceed332705",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}). Both-dead",
    "newString": "same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). Both-dead",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \\S\\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. For context: DAF $= 50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$, $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$, and $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (full curve in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). We treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic and DAF as a supplementary, environment-specific measure. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}\nDAF (90.4\\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \\gg$ typical non-death $\\Delta{\\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction, invariant to penalty magnitude, serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n",

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types (63\%) lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). Taxonomically redundant tasks share identical action vocabularies, reward functions, and environmental dynamics, differing only in target entity; we retain one representative per family (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}). This scope limits generalizability: conclusions apply to the included subset; increasing the 30-step budget might surface signal in excluded types (see Limitations). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types, with a mean $\Delta\text{DR} = +38.2$\,pp exceeding the included tasks' $+28.7$\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}). Extended validation at $n{=}20$ on five representative excluded tasks confirms this direction ($\overline{\Delta\text{DR}} = +32.0$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:extended-validation}). A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference between internal strategy variants ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}). Prescreening used $n{=}5$ episodes per task type; directional consistency (18/19 types favoring deterministic) rather than point estimates drives the inclusion criterion.
5	
6	\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}
7	\label{sec:death-decomp}
8	
9	\begin{figure}[t]
10	\centering
11	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/label_decomposition.pdf}
12	\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}
13	\label{fig:label-decomposition}
14	\end{figure}
15	
16	Of the 95 non-tie routing labels (Figure~\ref{fig:label-decomposition}), 75 (78.9\%; 95\% CI: $[70.7\%, 87.0\%]$, episode-level bootstrap) arise from death asymmetry: the internal strategy triggers a catastrophic $-100$ penalty while the deterministic strategy survives. This penalty-independent label fraction is the primary diagnostic metric. Of these, 54 are sole-signal labels where death was the only discriminator; at $P{=}0$ these reduce to ties, but the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}).
17	
18	Of 245 episodes, 150 (61.2\%) show no preference between strategies; routing is relevant in the remaining 95 (38.8\%). Among these, death avoidance dominates. The Death Avoidance Factor, a derived penalty-dependent measure, quantifies death's contribution to the total performance gap: DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 95\% CI$_{\text{nd}}$: $[0.34, 5.57]$; 95\% CI$_{\text{DAF}}$: $[81.3\%, 99.1\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF varies monotonically with $P$ (\S\ref{sec:daf}; Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\% label fraction provides the more robust characterization. When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} \to 0$, DAF $\to 1$ is algebraic necessity regardless of actual death dominance; conversely, when $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (as in Reflexion $N{\geq}3$; \S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), DAF exceeds 100\%, reflecting that death asymmetry more than accounts for the full gap.
19	
20	\begin{figure}[t]
```
