    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \\S\\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. For context: DAF $= 50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$, $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$, and $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (full curve in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). We treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic and DAF as a supplementary, environment-specific measure. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}\nDAF (90.4\\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \\gg$ typical non-death $\\Delta{\\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction, invariant to penalty magnitude, serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n",
        "numLines": 70,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 70
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/method.tex"
  },

    "content": {

      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nIn ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \\citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \\citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \\citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \\citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Always-deterministic matches 96.3\\% of oracle decisions, capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}); removing death episodes reveals that oracle access provides almost no non-death advantage ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 9.6\\% of total gap; \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}). Two independent 70B-class models yield DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\\%$ (Llama-70B exceeds 100\\%: death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap), and Reflexion ($N{=}3$) similarly drives DAF above 100\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\n\nThis addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\\% tie rate; \\S\\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\citep{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.\n\nCross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\\%$), four interventions, $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n",
        "numLines": 25,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 25
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/introduction.tex"
  },

        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Signal granularity and router failure.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property, but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories. Six router variants converge on near-chance accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$). Two a priori unpredictable results: $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models fails to eliminate death dominance (DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\\%$; Llama-70B exceeds 100\\%), and Reflexion yields DAF${}>100\\%$; cross-model DR stability ($36$--$49\\%$) confirms death vulnerability is a task-environment property.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and practical implications.}\nAlways-deterministic matches 96.3\\% of oracle decisions (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}), capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points. $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (9.6\\% of total gap) shows the advantage concentrates almost entirely in death episodes; ALFWorld (DAF${=}0\\%$) confirms the framework diagnoses qualitatively different mechanisms. A ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot suffices for build-vs-buy decisions (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}); action-space filtering reduces death rate by 19~pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). The decomposition requires binary catastrophic failures, asymmetric failure rates, and $P \\gg \\Delta_\\text{nd}$---ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH and ALFWorld lack the first.\n\n",
        "numLines": 10,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 10
      }
    },

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FehChkvj8Vjb7dWeqKEbGT`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types (63\%) lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). Taxonomically redundant tasks share identical action vocabularies, reward functions, and environmental dynamics, differing only in target entity; we retain one representative per family (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}). This scope limits generalizability: conclusions apply to the included subset; increasing the 30-step budget might surface signal in excluded types (see Limitations). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types, with a mean $\Delta\text{DR} = +38.2$\,pp exceeding the included tasks' $+28.7$\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}). Extended validation at $n{=}20$ on five representative excluded tasks confirms this direction ($\overline{\Delta\text{DR}} = +32.0$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:extended-validation}). A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference between internal strategy variants ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}). Prescreening used $n{=}5$ episodes per task type; directional consistency (18/19 types favoring deterministic) rather than point estimates drives the inclusion criterion.
5	
6	\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}
7	\label{sec:death-decomp}
8	
9	\begin{figure}[t]
10	\centering
11	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/label_decomposition.pdf}
12	\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}
13	\label{fig:label-decomposition}
14	\end{figure}
15	
16	Of the 95 non-tie routing labels (Figure~\ref{fig:label-decomposition}), 75 (78.9\%; 95\% CI: $[70.7\%, 87.0\%]$, episode-level bootstrap) arise from death asymmetry: the internal strategy triggers a catastrophic $-100$ penalty while the deterministic strategy survives. This penalty-independent label fraction is the primary diagnostic metric. Of these, 54 are sole-signal labels where death was the only discriminator; at $P{=}0$ these reduce to ties, but the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}).
17	
18	Of 245 episodes, 150 (61.2\%) show no preference between strategies; routing is relevant in the remaining 95 (38.8\%). Among these, death avoidance dominates. The Death Avoidance Factor, a derived penalty-dependent measure, quantifies death's contribution to the total performance gap: DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 95\% CI$_{\text{nd}}$: $[0.34, 5.57]$; 95\% CI$_{\text{DAF}}$: $[81.3\%, 99.1\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF varies monotonically with $P$ (\S\ref{sec:daf}; Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\% label fraction provides the more robust characterization. When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} \to 0$, DAF $\to 1$ is algebraic necessity regardless of actual death dominance; conversely, when $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (as in Reflexion $N{\geq}3$; \S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), DAF exceeds 100\%, reflecting that death asymmetry more than accounts for the full gap.
19	
20	\begin{figure}[t]
```

_source line 6025 · 2026-05-24T20:18:06.930Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RhodSteoH8hxrcSbLAdYiD`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\% confidence intervals for all metrics.
5	
6	\subsection{Problem Setup}
7	\label{sec:problem-setup}
8	
9	Consider an interactive environment with observation space $\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\pi: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$: \textbf{internal} (${\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \textbf{deterministic} (${\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \S\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \emph{routing policy} $\rho: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\rho$.
10	
```

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "d64d397e-0f88-47e8-9a08-aeb1e2cf29d9",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "404cbe1b-bec9-439a-b3f8-8d96e1919b94",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "417e4bd0-abe4-4a16-81ce-31cc66970b8a",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework",
    "newString": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/label_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}\n\\label{fig:label-decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur diagnostic framework",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \\S\\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. For context: DAF $= 50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$, $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$, and $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (full curve in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). We treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic and DAF as a supplementary, environment-specific measure. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}\nDAF (90.4\\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \\gg$ typical non-death $\\Delta{\\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction, invariant to penalty magnitude, serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 6,

          "+\\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}",
          "+\\label{fig:label-decomposition}",
          "+\\end{figure}",
          "+",
          " Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.",

Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BQKxrf9RCxFJvxLfmUtRtS`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types (63\%) lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). Taxonomically redundant tasks share identical action vocabularies, reward functions, and environmental dynamics, differing only in target entity; we retain one representative per family (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}). This scope limits generalizability: conclusions apply to the included subset; increasing the 30-step budget might surface signal in excluded types (see Limitations). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types, with a mean $\Delta\text{DR} = +38.2$\,pp exceeding the included tasks' $+28.7$\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}). Extended validation at $n{=}20$ on five representative excluded tasks confirms this direction ($\overline{\Delta\text{DR}} = +32.0$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:extended-validation}). A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference between internal strategy variants ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}). Prescreening used $n{=}5$ episodes per task type; directional consistency (18/19 types favoring deterministic) rather than point estimates drives the inclusion criterion.
5	
6	\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}
7	\label{sec:death-decomp}
8	
9	Of the 95 non-tie routing labels (Figure~\ref{fig:label-decomposition}), 75 (78.9\%; 95\% CI: $[70.7\%, 87.0\%]$, episode-level bootstrap) arise from death asymmetry: the internal strategy triggers a catastrophic $-100$ penalty while the deterministic strategy survives. This penalty-independent label fraction is the primary diagnostic metric. Of these, 54 are sole-signal labels where death was the only discriminator; at $P{=}0$ these reduce to ties, but the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}).
10	
11	Of 245 episodes, 150 (61.2\%) show no preference between strategies; routing is relevant in the remaining 95 (38.8\%). Among these, death avoidance dominates. The Death Avoidance Factor, a derived penalty-dependent measure, quantifies death's contribution to the total performance gap: DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 95\% CI$_{\text{nd}}$: $[0.34, 5.57]$; 95\% CI$_{\text{DAF}}$: $[81.3\%, 99.1\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF varies monotonically with $P$ (\S\ref{sec:daf}; Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\% label fraction provides the more robust characterization. When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} \to 0$, DAF $\to 1$ is algebraic necessity regardless of actual death dominance; conversely, when $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (as in Reflexion $N{\geq}3$; \S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), DAF exceeds 100\%, reflecting that death asymmetry more than accounts for the full gap.
12	
13	\begin{figure}[t]
14	\centering
15	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}
16	\caption{Per-task death rate differential vs.\ performance gap ($n{=}11$ task types). The near-perfect correlation ($r = 0.97$) is a structural consequence of death dominance (DAF $\to 1 \Rightarrow r \to 1$), not an independent finding. The sole outlier (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, red diamond) has zero deaths; its $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ arises entirely from non-death score differences.}
17	\label{fig:decomposition}
18	\end{figure}
19	
20	\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}
21	The internal strategy dies in 42.9\% of episodes versus 16.3\% for deterministic (2.6$\times$); a detailed failure-mode analysis reveals that all 105 internal-strategy deaths are triggered by the \texttt{focus} action (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}). Per-task death rate differential correlates with $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\ref{fig:decomposition}); since DAF$\to 1$ implies $r \to 1$ by construction, the near-perfect correlation is a consistency check, not an independent finding (Appendix~\ref{app:correlation}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \texttt{det\_preferred}, 9 \texttt{internal\_preferred}, and 150 \texttt{no\_preference}. Figure~\ref{fig:death-timeline} illustrates the typical death trajectory: agents execute long \texttt{connect} sequences before a terminal \texttt{focus on} triggers the $-100$ penalty.
22	
23	\begin{figure}[t]
24	\centering
25	\includegraphics[width=0.95\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_timeline.pdf}
26	\vspace{-2mm}
27	\caption{Action sequences for 6 representative death episodes. The fatal \texttt{focus on} (arrow) appears in final steps after long \texttt{connect} sequences (``connect loop $\to$ fatal focus'').}
28	\label{fig:death-timeline}
29	\end{figure}
30	
31	\paragraph{Signal concentration and binary structure.}
32	The Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\%$ of total death-rate differential. The aggregate 78.9\% reflects a per-task binary structure: all 10 death-prone task types produce 100\% death-asymmetry labels, while \texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths, $\Delta = +9.58$; Appendix~\ref{app:non-death-case-study}) produces 0\%. The framework thus diagnoses a binary property rather than a continuous routing signal. Removing \texttt{find-non-living-thing} raises the label fraction to 100\% and DAF $\geq$100\% (Appendix~\ref{app:loto-daf}).
33	
34	\paragraph{Sensitivity and seed robustness.}
35	Non-clustered episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\% CI $[81.3\%, 99.1\%]$ for DAF; cluster-robust CI $[73.3\%, 100.0\%]$ ($K{=}11$; Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Leave-one-task-out analysis confirms stability (label fraction range: 75.9\%--78.9\%; Appendix~\ref{app:loto-daf}); two independent replications---cross-model (Gemma-2-9B-it, DAF${}\,{=}\,89.7\%$; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}) and same-model with a different environment seed (seed=123, $N{=}220$, DAF${}\,{=}\,89.7\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness})---both converge within 0.7~pp. Exact permutation ($p=0.002$) and sign tests ($p<0.001$) on $K{=}11$ task-type clusters provide distribution-free confirmation (Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}; supplementary evidence in Appendix~\ref{app:per-task-ci}).
36	
37	\subsection{Multi-Model Validation (7--9B Tier)}
38	\label{sec:cross-model}
39	
40	A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\citep{llama3-2024} cross-model replication across all 11 task types ($N{=}220$) confirms that death dominance replicates across model families: internal death rate $41.4\%$ vs.\ deterministic $11.4\%$ (death-rate differential $+30.0$~pp), DAF $= 88.2\%$ (95\% CI: $[80.2\%, 95.1\%]$; $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +33.17$, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +3.9$), with direction consistency 10/10 on death-prone tasks, 83 deterministic-preferred, 3 internal-preferred (1.4\%, all death-asymmetry reversals), and 134 no-preference labels.
41	Both models show ${\sim}42\%$ internal vulnerability ($+26.5$ vs.\ $+30.0$~pp differential; per-task details in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}).
42	A third model family, Gemma-2-9B-it~\citep{gemma2-2024} (sliding window attention, vs.\ full attention in Qwen/Llama), independently confirms the pattern: DAF${=}89.7\%$ (95\% CI $[79.5\%, 98.4\%]$; $N{=}220$), with all 10 death-prone task types showing positive $\Delta$DR (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}). Three architecturally distinct models thus converge on DAF $88$--$90\%$ (spread 2.2~pp); $10{\times}$ scaling to two independent 70B-class models (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) confirms persistence---and intensification---across capability levels (DAF${=}92.4$--$101.0\%$).
43	
44	\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}
45	\label{sec:agent-improvement}
46	
47	\begin{table}[t]
48	\centering
49	\small
50	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
51	\toprule
52	Condition & Death\% & $N$ \\
53	\midrule
54	Single-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\
55	Reflexion ($3{\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\
56	Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\
57	Deterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\
58	\midrule
59	\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\
60	Death-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\
61	Retry ($5{\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\
62	\bottomrule
63	\end{tabular}
64	\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded). No intervention approaches deterministic-level safety.}
65	\label{tab:agent-improvement}
66	\end{table}
67	
68	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$ attempts without self-correction) yields marginal reduction on death-prone tasks ($43.5\%$ vs.\ $48.0\%$ baseline, $\Delta = -4.5$~pp). Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023}\footnote{Reflexion uses a fixed allocation of 20 episodes per task type ($20 \times 11 = 220$), vs.\ the baseline's variable 20--40 per type ($N{=}245$).} (verbal self-reflection between trials) reduces death to 31.4\% across all 11 tasks by rescuing 29.3\% of first-trial deaths, but the gap to deterministic remains 15.1~pp; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\sim}$35\%. Under Reflexion ($N{=}3$, matched-episode analysis), $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF $> 100\%$: the deterministic advantage is entirely attributable to death avoidance, and in non-death episodes the self-correcting agent slightly outperforms the deterministic baseline. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ parameters; both strategies independently collected at 72B, 255 episodes across all 11 task types) reduces death rate to $30.6\%$. The 72B deterministic strategy achieves $2.75\%$ death rate ($\Delta\text{DR} = +27.8$~pp), and $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.28$, yielding DAF $= 92.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[86.1\%, 97.9\%]$; 7B: $90.4\%$), with 5 internal-preferred episodes (vs.\ 9 at 7B; $2.0\%$ vs.\ $3.7\%$).
69	A second 70B-class model, Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct ($N{=}220$), intensifies the pattern: internal death rate $48.6\%$, deterministic $20.0\%$ ($\Delta\text{DR} = +28.6$~pp), $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = -0.30$, yielding DAF $= 101.0\%$ (95\% CI: $[95.3\%, 107.5\%]$). DAF exceeding 100\% indicates that death avoidance more than fully explains the deterministic advantage---when both agents survive, the internal agent performs comparably or slightly better. Label fraction rises to $91.3\%$ (67 det-preferred, 2 int-preferred; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}).
70	Two independent 70B-class models thus confirm that $10{\times}$ scaling does not attenuate death dominance; it intensifies proportional dominance because the deterministic strategy maintains substantially lower death rates than the internal strategy at both 70B scales ($2.75$--$20.0\%$ vs.\ $30.6$--$48.6\%$).
71	
72	\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}
73	We extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\% \to 43.5\%$). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p = 0.003$) then stabilizes ($34$--$36.5\%$ for $N \geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$); both the decline and plateau are well-powered (MDE ${\sim}8$~pp at 80\% power; Appendix~\ref{app:dose-response}).
74	
75	\begin{figure}[t]
76	\centering
77	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}
78	\caption{Death rate vs.\ trial count for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Reflexion drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus; Retry is nearly flat. Horizontal lines: 72B ($30.6\%$) and deterministic ($16.3\%$) baselines. Error bands: Wilson 95\% CIs.}
79	\label{fig:dose-response}
80	\end{figure}
81	
82	\subsection{Cross-Environment Boundary Probes}
83	\label{sec:math-contrast}
84	\label{sec:cross-env}
85	
86	We examine MATH and ALFWorld as boundary probes (not cross-domain validation). The deterministic oracle differs in information privilege across environments---ground-truth simulator (ScienceWorld), code interpreter (MATH), admissible-action enumeration (ALFWorld; \S\ref{sec:problem-setup})---so cross-environment results validate the diagnostic procedure's reusability, not the transferability of specific findings.
87	
88	\paragraph{MATH.} On MATH Level~4--5~\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$, code interpreter), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, McNemar $p = 0.81$), with 84.4\% no-preference. Without catastrophic failure, no routing signal emerges.
89	
90	\paragraph{ALFWorld.} ALFWorld~\citep{alfworld-2021} (6 task types, 135 games) provides the deterministic strategy with admissible actions, an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch, with no death mechanism ($\delta = 0$, DAF $= 0\%$). With Qwen2.5-7B, 35 of 37 non-tie labels (94.6\%) favor the information-advantaged strategy (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-env-details}); Llama-3.1-8B (135 games) yields 16 of 16 non-tie labels in the same direction (100\%). Llama shows fewer non-tie episodes (11.9\% vs.\ 25.9\%) but perfect directional consistency, confirming that information asymmetry, not death avoidance, drives routing signal in ALFWorld regardless of model family. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism (DAF $= 0\%$ vs.\ $90.4\%$) without modification. Figure~\ref{fig:signal-reversal} summarizes label distributions across all three environments.
91	
92	\begin{figure}[t]
93	\centering
94	\includegraphics[width=0.95\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}
95	\vspace{-2mm}
96	\caption{Cross-environment label distributions. ScienceWorld and ALFWorld both favor deterministic via different mechanisms (death avoidance vs.\ information asymmetry); MATH shows no routing signal.}
97	\label{fig:signal-reversal}
98	\end{figure}
99	
100	\subsection{Baselines}
101	\label{sec:baselines}
102	
103	\begin{table}[t]
104	\centering
105	\small
106	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
107	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
108	\toprule
109	Strategy & Score & 95\% CI & Death \% \\
110	\midrule
111	Always-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\
112	Always-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\
113	Random & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\
114	Oracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\
115	\midrule
116	Trained Router$^\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\
117	\bottomrule
118	\end{tabular}}
119	\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes). $\dagger$\,Single seed (42); see Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness.}
120	\label{tab:baselines}
121	\end{table}
122	
123	Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---an \emph{oracle degeneracy} where an always-deterministic lookup achieves 90.5\% of non-tie oracle decisions (balanced accuracy 50.0\%: perfect deterministic recall, zero internal recall). Even with perfect per-task death-risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform the always-deterministic baseline (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}).
124	
125	\subsection{Router Training: Inconclusive Results}
126	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
127	
128	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside. An implicit router (Qwen3, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA); Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$); episode-level independent evaluation yields $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$, below the majority-vote baseline.\footnote{The near-chance performance is consistent with noisy-label learning bounds~\citep{natarajan2013learning,northcutt2021confident}: ${\sim}$88\% of step-level labels are uninformative, class imbalance is extreme (90.5\% deterministic-preferred, 3.7\% minority), and paraphrase-based text augmentation (25\%--800\%) provides no improvement (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}).} Leave-one-task-type-out analysis degrades to chance (49.2\%; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}), as does a task-type-only classifier ($64.7\%$ in-distribution, $50.0\%$ leave-one-task-out (LOTO)), confirming routing signal is a per-task-type constant with no cross-type generalization. Deployed end-to-end, the best router scores $-10.70$ (95\% CI: $[-16.33, -5.27]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Results remain inconclusive under the current data regime ($N{=}245$, 3.7\% minority class).
129	
```

Under Reflexion ($N{=}3$, matched-episode analysis), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF $> 100\\%$: the deterministic advantage is entirely attributable to death avoidance, and in non-death episodes the self-correcting agent slightly outperforms the deterministic baseline. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ parameters; both strategies independently collected at 72B, 255 episodes across all 11 task types) reduces death rate to $30.6\\%$. The 72B deterministic strategy achieves $2.75\\%$ death rate ($\\Delta\\text{DR} = +27.8$~pp), and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.28$, yielding DAF $= 92.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[86.1\\%, 97.9\\%]$; 7B: $90.4\\%$), with 5 internal-preferred episodes (vs.\\ 9 at 7B; $2.0\\%$ vs.\\ $3.7\\%$).\nA second 70B-class model, Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct ($N{=}220$), intensifies the pattern: internal death rate $48.6\\%$, deterministic $20.0\\%$ ($\\Delta\\text{DR} = +28.6$~pp), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.30$, yielding DAF $= 101.0\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[95.3\\%, 107.5\\%]$). DAF exceeding 100\\% indicates that death avoidance more than fully explains the deterministic advantage---when both agents survive, the internal agent performs comparably or slightly better. Label fraction rises to $91.3\\%$ (67 det-preferred, 2 int-preferred; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\nTwo independent 70B-class models thus confirm that $10{\\times}$ scaling does not attenuate death dominance; it intensifies proportional dominance because the deterministic strategy maintains substantially lower death rates than the internal strategy at both 70B scales ($2.75$--$20.0\\%$ vs.\\ $30.6$--$48.6\\%$).\n\n\\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}\nWe extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\\% \\to 43.5\\%$). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p = 0.003$) then stabilizes ($34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$); both the decline and plateau are well-powered (MDE ${\\sim}8$~pp at 80\\% power; Appendix~\\ref{app:dose-response}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Death rate vs.\\ trial count for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Reflexion drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus; Retry is nearly flat. Horizontal lines: 72B ($30.6\\%$) and deterministic ($16.3\\%$) baselines. Error bands: Wilson 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:dose-response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Environment Boundary Probes}\n\\label{sec:math-contrast}\n\\label{sec:cross-env}\n\nWe examine MATH and ALFWorld as boundary probes (not cross-domain validation). The deterministic oracle differs in information privilege across environments---ground-truth simulator (ScienceWorld), code interpreter (MATH), admissible-action enumeration (ALFWorld; \\S\\ref{sec:problem-setup})---so cross-environment results validate the diagnostic procedure's reusability, not the transferability of specific findings.\n\n\\paragraph{MATH.} On MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$, code interpreter), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, McNemar $p = 0.81$), with 84.4\\% no-preference. Without catastrophic failure, no routing signal emerges.\n\n\\paragraph{ALFWorld.} ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} (6 task types, 135 games) provides the deterministic strategy with admissible actions, an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch, with no death mechanism ($\\delta = 0$, DAF $= 0\\%$). With Qwen2.5-7B, 35 of 37 non-tie labels (94.6\\%) favor the information-advantaged strategy (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-env-details}); Llama-3.1-8B (135 games) yields 16 of 16 non-tie labels in the same direction (100\\%). Llama shows fewer non-tie episodes (11.9\\% vs.\\ 25.9\\%) but perfect directional consistency, confirming that information asymmetry, not death avoidance, drives routing signal in ALFWorld regardless of model family. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism (DAF $= 0\\%$ vs.\\ $90.4\\%$) without modification. Figure~\\ref{fig:signal-reversal} summarizes label distributions across all three environments.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Cross-environment label distributions. ScienceWorld and ALFWorld both favor deterministic via different mechanisms (death avoidance vs.\\ information asymmetry); MATH shows no routing signal.}\n\\label{fig:signal-reversal}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Baselines}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Score & 95\\% CI & Death \\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\\\\nAlways-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\\\\nRandom & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\\\\nOracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained Router$^\\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes). $\\dagger$\\,Single seed (42); see Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nAlways-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---an \\emph{oracle degeneracy} where an always-deterministic lookup achieves 90.5\\% of non-tie oracle decisions (balanced accuracy 50.0\\%: perfect deterministic recall, zero internal recall). Even with perfect per-task death-risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform the always-deterministic baseline (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}).\n\n\\subsection{Router Training: Inconclusive Results}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nWe probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside. An implicit router (Qwen3, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA); Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); episode-level independent evaluation yields $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$, below the majority-vote baseline.\\footnote{The near-chance performance is consistent with noisy-label learning bounds~\\citep{natarajan2013learning,northcutt2021confident}: ${\\sim}$88\\% of step-level labels are uninformative, class imbalance is extreme (90.5\\% deterministic-preferred, 3.7\\% minority), and paraphrase-based text augmentation (25\\%--800\\%) provides no improvement (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).} Leave-one-task-type-out analysis degrades to chance (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}), as does a task-type-only classifier ($64.7\\%$ in-distribution, $50.0\\%$ leave-one-task-out (LOTO)), confirming routing signal is a per-task-type constant with no cross-type generalization. Deployed end-to-end, the best router scores $-10.70$ (95\\% CI: $[-16.33, -5.27]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Results remain inconclusive under the current data regime ($N{=}245$, 3.7\\% minority class).\n",

55	    \Delta_{\text{full}} \approx \underbrace{\delta \cdot P}_{\text{death contribution}} + \Delta_{\text{nd}}
56	\end{equation}
57	The approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\%$ of $\Delta_{\text{full}}$).
58	
59	We define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:
60	\begin{equation}
61	    \text{DAF} = 1 - \frac{\Delta_{\text{nd}}}{\Delta_{\text{full}}} \approx \frac{\delta P}{\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}}
62	\end{equation}%
63	\footnote{When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}
64	
65	Since DAF $= \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. For context: DAF $= 50.4\%$ at $P{=}10$, $82.6\%$ at $P{=}50$, and $90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ (full curve in Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). We treat the label fraction (78.9\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic and DAF as a supplementary, environment-specific measure. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}).
66	
67	\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}
68	DAF (90.4\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \gg$ typical non-death $\Delta{\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction, invariant to penalty magnitude, serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.
69	
70	\paragraph{Statistical inference.}
71	We quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}.
72	
73	We also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}), reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.
74	
75	\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}
76	At step level, 97.4\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\S\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
77

          " Of 245 episodes, 150 (61.2\\%) show no preference between strategies; routing is relevant in the remaining 95 (38.8\\%). Among these, death avoidance dominates. The Death Avoidance Factor, a derived penalty-dependent measure, quantifies death's contribution to the total performance gap: DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 95\\% CI$_{\\text{nd}}$: $[0.34, 5.57]$; 95\\% CI$_{\\text{DAF}}$: $[81.3\\%, 99.1\\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF varies monotonically with $P$ (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}; Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\\% label fraction provides the more robust characterization. When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} \\to 0$, DAF $\\to 1$ is algebraic necessity regardless of actual death dominance; conversely, when $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (as in Reflexion $N{\\geq}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), DAF exceeds 100\\%, reflecting that death asymmetry more than accounts for the full gap.",

          " The internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic (2.6$\\times$); a detailed failure-mode analysis reveals that all 105 internal-strategy deaths are triggered by the \\texttt{focus} action (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). Per-task death rate differential correlates with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}); since DAF$\\to 1$ implies $r \\to 1$ by construction, the near-perfect correlation is a consistency check, not an independent finding (Appendix~\\ref{app:correlation}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, 9 \\texttt{internal\\_preferred}, and 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference}. Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\nReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\\\\nDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\nDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\nRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under improvement methods.     "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/label_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}\n\\label{fig:label-decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \\S\\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. For context: DAF $= 50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$, $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$, and $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (full curve in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). We treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic and DAF as a supplementary, environment-specific measure. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}\nDAF (90.4\\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \\gg$ typical non-death $\\Delta{\\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction, invariant to penalty magnitude, serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UDm1yBb6aasnjRgjZtq6Pz`</summary>

```text
30	Each trajectory $\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\tau) \in \mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\tau) \in \{0, 1\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:
31	\begin{multline}
32	    \bigl(s^{\tt int}, s^{\tt det}, d^{\tt int}, d^{\tt det}\bigr) \triangleq \\
33	    \bigl(s(\tau_{\tt int}),\, s(\tau_{\tt det}),\, d(\tau_{\tt int}),\, d(\tau_{\tt det})\bigr)
34	\end{multline}
35	
36	We use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \S\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\ref{sec:cross-env}.
37	
38	\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}
39	\label{sec:labeling}
40	
41	We define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:
42	\begin{multline}
43	    L\!\bigl(\tau_{\tt int}^{(t)}, \tau_{\tt det}^{(t)}\bigr) \;\rightarrow \\
44	    \ell \in \{{\tt det\text{-}pref},\; {\tt int\text{-}pref},\; {\tt no\text{-}pref}\}
45	\end{multline}
46	
47	The labeling function is \emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \texttt{no\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.
48	
49	
```

</details>
