Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\nReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\\\\nDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\nDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\nRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded). No intervention approaches deterministic-level safety.}\n\\label{tab:agent-improvement}\n\\end{table}",
    "newString": "At step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/label_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}\n\\label{fig:label-decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \\S\\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. For context: DAF $= 50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$, $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$, and $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (full curve in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). We treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic and DAF as a supplementary, environment-specific measure. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}\nDAF (90.4\\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \\gg$ typical non-death $\\Delta{\\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction, invariant to penalty magnitude, serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\nReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\\\\nDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\nDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\nRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded). No intervention approaches deterministic-level safety.}\n\\label{tab:agent-improvement}\n\\end{table}\n",

Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. For context: DAF $= 50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$, $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$, and $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (full curve in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). We treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic and DAF as a supplementary, environment-specific measure. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}\nDAF (90.4\\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \\gg$ typical non-death $\\Delta{\\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction, invariant to penalty magnitude, serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n",
